
Recommendation adherence across day types and Brown windows
Main analysis with a separate exploratory cross-state extension






    
    
    Published
    
      September 12, 2026

ImportantAnswer in brief



Recommendation adherence was lower on Free days than Work days during Daytime and Sleep. Pre-sleep adherence was higher in the primary analysis, but its confidence interval included zero after requiring at least 80% coverage. The Pre-sleep finding therefore needs a coverage qualification.
The separate cross-state extension found limited inverse associations between participants’ average Daytime adherence and their Sleep or Pre-sleep adherence. Its day-level question remains unresolved because temporal dependence could not be adequately addressed. Neither analysis establishes causation or stable participant types.


1 Main analysis: adherence across Work and Free days
1.1 Question and measurement

Among valid observed minutes, how often was the Brown recommendation met, and how did adherence differ across Work and Free days, recommendation windows, and sites?

Daytime, Pre-sleep and Sleep identify the Brown et al. recommendation windows. Melanopic equivalent daylight illuminance (melEDI) is light weighted for melanopsin-related sensitivity, measured in lux. A valid minute was adherent at melEDI at least 250 lx during Daytime, at most 10 lx in the three hours before sleep, or at most 1 lx during Sleep. Threshold equality counted as adherent.
Each cycle links the preceding Sleep, Daytime, and following three-hour Pre-sleep windows. Daytime excludes Pre-sleep. The wake-start calendar date determines Work or Free day; midnight does not split the cycle. Actual diary dates and UTC interval boundaries establish chronology, without bridging diary gaps.
Adherence is adherent valid minutes divided by all valid minutes in a window period. Each window qualifies independently. The primary analysis retains periods with at least one valid comparison; the sensitivity analysis requires at least 80% of the expected interval to contain valid data. Expected duration defines coverage, not the model denominator. Missing minutes are not imputed, and exact 0% and 100% periods remain included.
Near-eye measurements are primary during Daytime and Pre-sleep. Sleep describes the device-recorded bedside environment near the sleeper, not ocular exposure or a whole-bedroom measurement.
1.2 Model and exact samples
The endpoint-inflated beta-binomial model represents the adherent and non-adherent minute counts, allows extra wholly non-adherent or wholly adherent periods, and accounts for overdispersion and repeated observations within participants. It includes the complete window-by-site-by-day-type interaction and a participant random intercept. Site-average estimates give each of nine sites equal weight; they are not pooled-minute fractions.
A 95% confidence interval (CI) describes uncertainty under the model. A false-discovery-rate (FDR) adjustment addresses multiple tests within a defined family. Whether a CI excludes zero and whether an FDR-adjusted test meets its threshold are distinct summaries.



Table 1: Exact main-analysis samples; windows qualify independently.

1.3 Main window, day-type, and site findings
The estimates below summarize recommendation adherence, with separate Work-day and Free-day values. Absolute levels should be interpreted within each window because its threshold and direction differ.




Figure 1: Site-average recommendation adherence on Work and Free days. Daytime, Pre-sleep and Sleep identify the Brown et al. recommendation windows. Each point gives equal weight to nine sites; bars are 95% confidence intervals. Sleep reflects bedside measurements.

Source data for Figure 1



Table 2: Equal-site recommendation-adherence levels and 95% CIs in both samples.

The primary differences are -5.1 pp for Daytime, +5.9 pp for Pre-sleep, and -6.5 pp for Sleep. These are differences in percentage points, not proportional percentage changes.
The global interaction and the window-specific site-by-day-type tests assess different questions: whether site differences in the Free-minus-Work pattern vary across windows, and whether each window’s Free-minus-Work difference varies across sites.

Display source; Unrounded source 1; Unrounded source 2





Figure 2: Work-day recommendation adherence by site. Daytime, Pre-sleep and Sleep identify the Brown et al. recommendation windows. Points and bars show adherence levels and 95% CIs. Filled diamonds mark the separate stored site-minus-equal-site Work-day tests that met the FDR threshold. The long-dashed blue line is the equal-site Work-day mean, including the indexed site. All estimates come from one pooled model; sites are not independent replications or causal effects of location.


Source data for Figure 2
The next figure distinguishes two tests. A filled diamond identifies a site’s Free-minus-Work difference from zero. An asterisk identifies a difference from the equal-site Free-minus-Work reference. These use separate FDR families. The indexed site is included in its own equal-site reference.





Figure 3: Site-specific Free-minus-Work recommendation-adherence differences. Daytime, Pre-sleep and Sleep identify the Brown et al. recommendation windows. Bars are 95% CIs. Filled diamonds mark FDR-supported differences from zero; asterisks mark FDR-supported departures from the window-specific equal-site effect in a separate family. The dotted line is zero and the long-dashed blue line is the equal-site effect, including the indexed site. All 27 results are shown from one pooled interaction model, not independent site replications or causal effects.


Source data for Figure 3



Table 5: Work-day adherence, Free-day differences and site-specific deviations from the equal-site average.

1.4 Endpoint calibration and coverage sensitivity
Endpoint calibration checks whether wholly non-adherent and wholly adherent periods are represented adequately. Both primary samples passed all five applicable window-level predictive-envelope checks. Overall adherence calibration was acceptable with limitations: temporal dependence remained triggered in both samples. Endpoint calibration does not resolve that separate limitation.



Table 6: Observed endpoint counts and their stored conditional predictive envelopes.

Daytime and Sleep retain direction and interval conclusion. Pre-sleep retains its positive direction but not CI exclusion of zero, so it does not meet the prespecified coverage criterion. This does not establish absence of an association, nor does it test whether the two estimates differ.
1.5 Retained random effect and descriptive decomposition
The retained participant intercept allows people to differ in baseline adherence after fixed adjustment. Its logit-scale standard deviation is a model parameter, not a response-scale percentage. Participant window slopes, day-type slopes, a cycle intercept and endpoint-process intercepts were not retained after structural failures. Their inactive placeholders are not zero-variance estimates.



Table 8: The sole retained random effect in each main sample.

Display source; Unrounded source 1
Shapley allocation distributes fixed-effect variation across predictors while averaging over the possible orders in which their information is considered. Global differences between recommendation windows account for most fixed-effect variation; this partly reflects their different thresholds. Within-window allocations instead compare site and day-type contributions. These are descriptive properties of the fitted model, not causal importance or a participant ranking.

All decomposition results are point-only. Fifteen- versus thirty-node integration met the prespecified numerical agreement criterion; this numerical check is not a confidence interval.
1.6 Complementary chest measurements
The chest analysis is a separate sample of 153 participants, 1,689 Daytime or Pre-sleep periods, 861 cycles and eight sites. It is not nested by assumption within the 140-participant primary sample and is not pooled with it. Equal weighting therefore uses eight sites, not the primary nine. The two contrast directions and interval conclusions were retained. Endpoint calibration passed three applicable checks, while overall calibration and temporal dependence require qualifications. There is no chest Sleep estimate and no paired sensor-position difference or equivalence test.



Table 12: Complementary chest versus primary Free-minus-Work summaries.

Free-minus-Work contrasts. The diary-indexed grouping associates the Pre-sleep interval with the indexed sleep record rather than the following evening. This is a grouping sensitivity, not a test of a difference between model estimates.





Display source; Unrounded source 1
The actual-date temporal endpoint models failed structural checks. The first eligible beta-binomial temporal fallback in each sample changed both distributional family and random-effects structure and retained near-boundary cautions. Its Free-minus-Work directions and interval conclusions agreed with the corresponding primary sample, but endpoint calibration failed all five applicable checks in both fallback samples. The any-valid fallback retained a Daytime residual-correlation trigger. The absence of a registered trigger in the coverage fallback does not establish independence. These fallbacks do not replace the primary model.
All nine site-deletion checks and four eligible participant-deletion checks are preserved. A fifth participant deletion failed its gradient check and has no derived inference. The ordinary-binomial diagnostic model also failed. The calendar-day comparator retained a variance-boundary caution. Pre-sleep interval conclusions were also sensitive to 70% coverage, 90% coverage and restricting to participants observed on both day types; the last two exceeded the two-percentage-point shift criterion.






NoteComplete recorded sensitivity results





The following tables retain the completed checks and failures. Unsuccessful routes are not interpreted as evidence of no effect.

1.8 Main-analysis limitations
The any-valid estimand concerns observed valid minutes, not unmeasured portions of each window. The 80% restriction changes which periods contribute and qualifies Pre-sleep. Residual temporal dependence, near-boundary sensitivity fits and failed diagnostic routes constrain precision claims. The site comparisons describe one pooled observational model, not independent replication or causal site effects. The recommendations and measurement positions differ by window, especially for bedside Sleep.
2 Exploratory cross-state extension
The extension is subordinate to the main analysis. It asks whether Daytime adherence aligns with Sleep and Pre-sleep adherence within the same behavioral cycles and across participants. It does not replace or independently replicate the main Free-minus-Work analysis.
2.1 What was compared
Each behavioral cycle began with Sleep, continued through Daytime, and ended with Pre-sleep. Its Work or Free classification came from the calendar day on which Daytime began. Daytime adherence was the predictor. Sleep and Pre-sleep adherence were the two outcomes.
The primary model contained 1,376 Sleep or Pre-sleep outcome rows from 140 participants and 761 Daytime-anchored cycles. The pairwise eligibility sample uses 758 Daytime-Sleep and 618 Daytime-Pre-sleep pairs. It is not every potential pair in the main model; the latter also contains Pre-sleep periods outside this extension’s eligible pair set. Exact 0% and 100% adherence values remained part of the endpoint-inflated model.
The selected endpoint-inflated beta-binomial model retained a participant random intercept representing participant-level variation and adjusted for state, site, Work or Free day, their prespecified interactions, and each participant’s mix of Daytime observations across day types. The association-cycle intercept could not be retained, so serial dependence was assessed separately rather than treated as resolved by the random-effects structure.



Table 18: Exact samples used by the selected cross-state models.

Source data for Table 18
The analysis separated two questions:
Within participant: when one person’s Daytime adherence was higher than usual for that person, was adherence in Sleep or Pre-sleep also different?
Between participants: did participants with higher observed average Daytime adherence also have different average Sleep or Pre-sleep adherence?
2.2 Participant profiles across recommendation windows
For the descriptive profile display, Daytime adherence was first deduplicated so that each participant-cycle contributed only one Daytime value. Each plotted value is then that participant’s unweighted mean across their observed cycles for the given recommendation window. Of 140 participants in the primary frame, 139 had observations in all three windows and contribute three points each.




Figure 5: Participant-average recommendation adherence across the Brown et al. recommendation windows. Daytime, Pre-sleep, and Sleep are the window labels. Each colored point is one anonymous participant’s equal-cycle mean within that window. Faint lines connect the same anonymous participant across the three window summaries; they are not time trajectories or ranks. Half-violin shapes show the window distributions, and dark bars with white points show interquartile ranges and medians. Because each window has its own Brown et al. threshold and direction, absolute percentages should be interpreted within window.

Source data for Figure 5
The absolute adherence percentages differ markedly among windows because the Brown recommendation, threshold direction, and behavioral context differ by window. The connecting lines therefore show paired participant profiles, not a single scale of overall performance.
The medians and interquartile ranges summarized in the figure are shown below.



Table 19: Observed participant-average adherence in the complete three-state display.

2.3 Adjusted cross-state associations
The model adjusted the two association levels simultaneously, together with state, site, Work or Free day, their prespecified interactions, and the composition of each participant’s Daytime observations across day types. Effects below are response-scale percentage-point differences per 10 percentage points higher Daytime adherence. Sites receive equal weight, and Work and Free days receive equal weight.



Table 20: Adjusted Sleep and Pre-sleep differences per 10 percentage points higher Daytime adherence.

A false-discovery-rate (FDR) adjustment covered all four displayed effects; the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure is retained in the detailed methods.





Source data for Table 20
2.3.1 Day-level question
A 10-point higher-than-usual Daytime value corresponded to -0.3 pp Sleep adherence (95% CI -1.0 pp to +0.4 pp) and -1.1 pp Pre-sleep adherence (95% CI -2.7 pp to +0.6 pp). Both intervals include zero.
These intervals alone would indicate that the data did not establish a day-level association. In addition, residuals remained positively correlated between successive observed dates for both outcomes. The prespecified temporal sensitivity analyses could not all be retained. A day-level scientific claim is therefore withheld rather than interpreted as evidence that no relationship exists.
2.3.2 Participant-level question
Participants with 10 points higher observed average Daytime adherence had an estimated 2.5 pp lower Sleep adherence (95% CI 1.5 pp to 3.6 pp lower) and 3.6 pp lower Pre-sleep adherence (95% CI 1.2 pp to 5.9 pp lower). Both 95% confidence intervals excluded zero, and both tests passed the separate four-effect false-discovery-rate threshold.
This is an observed between-participant association after the prespecified adjustment. It does not show that increasing one person’s Daytime adherence would reduce that person’s Sleep or Pre-sleep adherence.
2.4 Descriptive Daytime-cycle groups, not participant rankings
As a descriptive check, individual behavioral cycles were assigned to low, middle, or high groups using that cycle’s within-participant Daytime deviation from the participant’s observed mean. The cutpoints were cohort-relative tertiles, and ties stayed together. These are cycle groups, not participant groups: one participant can contribute different cycles to different groups. The adjusted target-adherence values are point estimates only. No category p-values were calculated.



Table 21: Point-only adjusted target adherence across cohort-relative Daytime-cycle groups.

Source data for Table 21
Across the any-valid cycle groups, adjusted Sleep adherence changed from 88.0% in the low group to 87.3% in the high group, and adjusted Pre-sleep adherence changed from 65.5% to 62.9%. The at-least-80% display had the same shallow direction. This descriptive pattern does not overcome the unresolved temporal dependence and does not establish a day-level association.
2.5 Model checks and sensitivity analyses
The at-least-80% coverage sensitivity analysis preserved the direction and interval-exclusion status of all four effects. The largest response-scale change was 0.60 percentage points.
The endpoint-calibration model check was acceptable in both samples: all six target-level all-no, mixed, and all-yes predictive envelopes passed. Overall adherence calibration was acceptable with limitations. The largest absolute difference among target-by-site-by-day-type cells with at least 10 rows was 6.3 percentage points in the any-valid sample and 6.4 percentage points after the 80% requirement. Endpoint calibration therefore passed, but it does not resolve the separate temporal limitation.
Source data for the model-check assessment



Table 22: Stability after requiring at least 80% coverage within state periods.

Source data for Table 22
The balanced Work and Free day sensitivity analysis also retained the participant-level directions and interval decisions. Among bounded influence checks, five of nine leave-one-site-out fits and four of five participant-deletion fits remained estimable under the unchanged endpoint model. All successfully estimated sensitivity models preserved the participant-level directions and interval decisions, but the incomplete set limits claims about robustness to every site or participant.
2.6 Cross-state interpretation and limitations
The strongest supported conclusion is narrow: within this cohort and observed monitoring window, participants with higher observed average Daytime adherence tended to have modestly lower Sleep and Pre-sleep adherence after adjustment.
Several qualifications matter:
The association is observational and non-causal.
A participant’s observed mean over several cycles is not an error-free stable trait.
The three states use different Brown thresholds and directions, so their absolute percentages are not interchangeable measures of overall behavior.
Positive serial dependence remains unresolved for the day-level question.
The site and participant influence checks were incomplete because some unchanged endpoint models were not estimable.
The raincloud points are anonymous paired summaries. They do not assign participants to high, medium, or low categories and cannot support ranking or tertile-overlap claims.
3 Integrated interpretation
The main analysis describes recommendation adherence across Work and Free days. Lower Free-day adherence during Daytime and Sleep is retained under the coverage restriction; higher Pre-sleep adherence in the primary sample needs an explicit coverage qualification.
The exploratory extension asks a different question: whether Daytime adherence aligns with the other windows within cycles or between participants. Its day-level claim remains withheld, and its participant-level result is a limited inverse association. Neither the main day-type pattern nor the exploratory associations establish stable adherence types or a biological or behavioral trade-off. The overlapping observations are not independent replication.
4 Detailed methods and provenance
The preparation and provenance companion explains the measurement, weighting, samples, model checks and separate testing families. The implementation and reconciliation report retains the complete technical execution and failure history. The exploratory model is documented in its implementation report.
This report reads frozen aggregate outputs. The four main figures display the current model outputs; the anonymous participant raincloud is exact reuse. No model fitting, new inference or reclassification occurs in report execution.
